Accelerated Extragradient-Type Methods: Generalization and Unified Convergence Analysis
Quoc Tran-Dinh, Nghia Nguyen-Trung

Abstract
This paper provides a comprehensive study for two types of extragradient methods: anchored extragradient-type and Nesterov’s accelerated extragradient-type for solving [non]linear inclusions (and, in particular, equations), primarily under the Lipschitz continuity and the co-hypomonotonicity assumptions. It consists of the following main contributions. First, we unify and generalize a class of anchored extragradient methods for monotone inclusions to a wider range of schemes encompassing existing algorithms as special cases. We establish 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 / k ) \mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big) last-iterate convergence rates on the residual norm of the underlying mapping for this general framework and then specialize it to obtain convergence guarantees for specific instances, where k k denotes the iteration counter. Second, we extend our approach to a class of anchored Tseng’s forward-backward-forward splitting (FBFS) methods to obtain a broader class of algorithms for solving co-hypomonotone inclusions, covering a class of nonmonotone problems. Again, we prove 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 / k ) \mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big) last-iterate convergence rates for this general scheme and specialize it to obtain convergence results for existing and new variants. Third, we develop a new class of moving anchor FBFS algorithms that achieves both 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 / k ) \mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big) and o ⁡ ( 1 / k ) o\big(1/k\big) convergence rates, and the convergence of iterate sequences. Fourth, we generalize and unify Nesterov’s accelerated extra-gradient method to a new class of algorithms that covers existing schemes as special instances while generating new variants. For these schemes, we can prove 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 / k ) \mathcal{O}\left(1/k\right) last-iterate convergence rates for the residual norm under co-hypomonotonicity. Fifth, we propose another novel class of Nesterov’s accelerated extragradient methods to solve inclusions. Interestingly, these algorithms achieve both 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 / k ) \mathcal{O}\left(1/k\right) and o ⁡ ( 1 / k ) o\left(1/k\right) last-iterate convergence rates, and also the convergence of iterate sequences under co-hypomonotonicity and Lipschitz continuity. Finally, we provide a set of numerical experiments encompassing different scenarios to validate our results.



∎

MSC
90C25 90C06 90-08

1
Introduction

Overview.

The
generalized equation
, also known as the
nonlinear inclusion
, serves as a versatile mathematical tool for modeling a wide range of problems in computational mathematics and related disciplines. It encompasses optimization problems (both constrained and unconstrained), minimax optimization, variational inequalities, complementarity problems, game theory, and fixed-point problems, see, e.g.,
[
9
,
17
,
28
,
61
,
64
,
66
,
68
]
.
This model has found direct applications in diverse fields such as operations research, economics, uncertainty quantification, and transportation, see, e.g.,
[
12
,
29
,
35
,
28
,
38
]
.

In recent years, there has been a surge of interest in minimax problems, a special case of generalized equations, driven by their applications in machine learning and robust optimization. This is particularly evident in the areas of generative adversarial networks (GANs), adversarial training, and distributionally robust optimization, see, e.g.,
[
2
,
12
,
31
,
41
,
45
,
63
]
.
Furthermore, minimax models have emerged as valuable tools in online learning and reinforcement learning, see, e.g.,
[
2
,
7
,
13
,
31
,
36
,
41
,
43
,
45
,
63
,
84
]
.
The growing prominence of these applications has spurred renewed research efforts in nonlinear inclusions, fixed-point problems, and operator equations.

1.1
Problem statements, equivalent forms, and special cases

\mathrm{(a)}
Problem statement.

In this paper, we consider the following
generalized equation
(also known as a [composite]
[non]linear inclusion
):

\textrm{Find $x^{\star}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)$ such that:}\quad 0\in\Phi x^{\star}\equiv Fx^{\star}+Tx^{\star},

(NI)

where
F:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
is a single-valued operator,
T:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightrightarrows 2^{\mathbb{R}^{p}}
is a set-valued (or multivalued) mapping from
\mathbb{R}^{p}
to
2^{\mathbb{R}^{p}}
(the set of all subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
),
\Phi:=F+T
, and
\mathrm{dom}(\Phi):=\mathrm{dom}(F)\cap\mathrm{dom}(T)
is the domain of
\Phi
, which is the intersection of the domains of
F
and
T
.
We denote
\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
the solution set of (
NI
).

In this paper, we work with finite-dimensional Euclidean spaces
\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Nevertheless, we believe that most results presented in this paper can be extended to Hilbert spaces.
This work is also a continuation of our recent paper
[
81
]
on non-accelerated extragradient-type methods.

\mathrm{(b)}
Special cases.

Problem (
NI
) covers the following special cases.

\mathrm{(i)}

If
T=0
, then (
NI
) reduces to a
[non]linear equation
:

\textrm{Find $x^{\star}\in\mathrm{dom}(F)$ such that:}\quad Fx^{\star}=0.

(NE)

Though (
NE
) is a special case of (
NI
), in many cases, (
NI
) can be reformulated equivalently to (
NE
) via, e.g., Moreau-Yosida’s approximation or forward-backward splitting residual operator, see, e.g.,
[
80
]
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

Note that (
NE
) is equivalent to the following fixed-point problem:

\textrm{Find $x^{\star}\in\mathrm{dom}(G)$ such that:}\quad x^{\star}=Gx^{\star},

(FixP)

where
G:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
is a given operator.
Clearly, by defining
Fx:=x-Gx
, then (
FixP
) is equivalent to (
NE
).
Alternatively, we can rewrite (
NI
) equivalently to Kakutani’s fixed-point problem:
Find
x^{\star}
such that
x^{\star}\in\Psi{x^{\star}}
in the context of multivalued mappings, where
\Psi:=\mathbb{I}-\Phi
.

\mathrm{(iii)}

If
T:=\partial{g}
, the subdifferential of a proper, closed, and convex function
g:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
, then (
NI
) reduces a
mixed variational inequality problem
(MVIP):
Find
x^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that:

\langle Fx^{\star},x-x^{\star}\rangle+g(x)-g(x^{\star})\geq 0,\ \forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}.

(MVIP)

In particular, if
T=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}}
, the normal cone of a nonempty, closed, and convex set
\mathcal{X}
in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
(i.e.
g=\delta_{\mathcal{X}}
, the indicator of
\mathcal{X}
), then (
MVIP
) reduces to the classical (Stampacchia’s)
variational inequality problem
(VIP):

\textrm{Find $x^{\star}\in\mathcal{X}$ such that:}\quad\langle Fx^{\star},x-x^{\star}\rangle\geq 0,\ \textrm{for all}\ x\in\mathcal{X}.

(VIP)

While (
VIP
) can be viewed as a primal VIP (or a strong VIP), its dual (or weak) form can be written as

\textrm{Find $x^{\star}\in\mathcal{X}$ such that:}\quad\langle Fx,x-x^{\star}\rangle\geq 0,\ \textrm{for all}\ x\in\mathcal{X},

(DVIP)

which is known as Minty’s variational inequality problem.
If
F
is monotone (see the definition in Section
2
) then both problems (
VIP
) and (
MVIP
) are equivalent, i.e. their solution sets are identical, see
[
28
,
38
]
.

\mathrm{(iv)}

One important special case of (
NI
) or (
VIP
) is the optimality condition of the following general minimax problem:

\min_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}\max_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big\{\mathcal{L}(u,v):=\varphi(u)+\mathcal{H}(u,v)-\psi(v)\Big\},

(1)

where
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
and
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
are often proper, closed, and convex functions, and
\mathcal{H}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is a joint objective function, often assumed to be differentiable, but not necessarily convex-concave.
If we denote
x:=[u,v]
as the concatenation of
u
and
v
, and define
T:=[\partial{\varphi},\partial{\psi}]
and
F:=[\nabla_{u}{\mathcal{H}}(u,v),-\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{H}}(u,v)]
, then the optimality condition of (
1
) is exactly covered by (
NI
) as a special case.

Since (
NI
) covers the above problems as special cases or equivalent forms, our methods developed in this paper can be customized to solve these problems.

1.2
Our goals and contributions

Before discussing related work, let us state our goals and contributions in this paper.
We also compare our work with the most related and recent works.

\mathrm{(a)}
Our goals.

Our first goal is to unify and generalize both Halpern’s and Nesterov’s accelerated extragradient-type methods, covering existing algorithms as special cases.
Our second goal is to develop new classes of accelerated extragradient algorithms to solve (
NI
) with “better” theoretical guarantees.
We establish convergence rates for these generalized methods under standard or weaker assumptions.
Unlike classical methods using gap functions or a distance to the solution set
\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
, our convergence metric is the residual norm (
cf.
(
2
)), which is applicable to both monotone and nonmonotone problems, and requires mild assumptions.
We also prove the convergence of iterate sequences to a solution of (
NI
).
Our approach allows for the generation of new variants through flexible choices for a general search direction, denoted by
u^{k}
.

\mathrm{(b)}
Our contributions.

Our contributions are detailed below, and Table
1
offers a brief overview of our results and their distinction from prior results.

\mathrm{1.}

Generalized Anchored EG.

We generalize the extra-anchored gradient method to a broader class of algorithms for solving (
NI
).
Under a “monotonicity” assumption on
F
and a
3
-cyclically monotone assumption of
T
, we prove
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)
-last-iterate sublinear convergence rates for this generalized scheme, where
k
denotes the iteration counter.
This framework subsumes several common variants and known schemes in the literature as special instances (see Table
1
), while allowing us to derive new variants.

\mathrm{2.}

Generalized Fast EG.

We generalize the fast EG method in
[
40
]
for solving (
NI
) to a broader class of schemes and establish its
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)
-sublinear last-iterate convergence rate under a “co-hypomonotonicity” assumption of
\Phi
and the Lipschitz continuity of
F
.
This generalization encompasses the fast EG method in
[
40
]
and the past-fast EG in
[
19
]
as special instances.

\mathrm{3.}

Generalized Moving Anchor EG.

We propose a class of generalized moving anchor EG methods for solving (
NI
).
This class covers the methods in
[
1
,
88
]
as special cases and again allows us to derive new variants, including a new variant of the optimistic gradient scheme.
We establish both
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)
and
o\big(1/k\big)
last-iterate convergence rates under co-hypomonotonicity of
\Phi
and Lipschitz continuity of
F
.
We also prove the convergence of iterate sequences to a solution of (
NI
).

\mathrm{4.}

Generalized Nesterov’s Accelerated EG.

We study a generalized form of Nesterov’s accelerated EG method, inspired by the connection to Halpern’s methods in
[
78
]
.
We provide a unified
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k\right)
-last-iterate convergence rate for this generalized method, which covers Tseng’s accelerated variant and Nesterov’s accelerated forward-reflected-backward method (also known as an accelerated optimistic gradient scheme).

\mathrm{5.}

Generalized Nesterov’s Accelerated EG with Better Guarantees.

We develop a new class of Nesterov’s accelerated EG methods using different correction terms to solve (
NI
).
These methods appear to have better convergence guarantees than the previous one, and encompass
[
15
,
71
]
as special cases but under the co-hypomonotone setting.
We prove both
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k\right)
and
o\left(1/k\right)
convergence rates through flexible parameter updates.
In addition, we also establish the convergence of iterate sequences to a solution of (
NI
), which have not been proven in the previous methods.

=

APEG

=
Nesterov’s accelerated past extragradient;
and the prefix
G
is for
Generalized
(i.e. our methods).
In addition,
v^{k-1}
is an appropriate direction depending on the method;

mono

=
monotone;

chm

=
co-hypomonotone;
and
3-cm

=

3
-cyclically monotone.
Note that “Add. Ass.” means “Additional Assumptions”; and “
ISC
” abbreviates for “iterate sequence convergence”.

\mathrm{(b)}
Comparison.

We believe that our results in this paper are new and significant.
Indeed, let us compare them with the most related works here.

\bullet

First, one key novelty lies in our search directions, denoted by
u^{k}
, as illustrated in Figure
1
.
While classical extragradient methods and their accelerated variants specifically employ two search directions:
Fx^{k}
and
Fy^{k}
evaluated at two distinct sequences
\{x^{k}\}
and
\{y^{k}\}
, past-extragradient and optimistic gradient-type schemes reduce the number of
F
evaluations by replacing
Fx^{k}
by
Fy^{k-1}
, enabling one to eliminate the sequence
\{x^{k}\}
.
Our GEG-type methods utilize
u^{k}
and
Fy^{k}
, where
u^{k}
is a flexible linear combination of
Fx^{k}
,
Fy^{k-1}
, and potentially a prior computed quantity
v^{k-1}
.
This flexibility expands the spectrum of our methods and offers a potential for improving practical performance by searching for the “best” direction
u^{k}
among various linear combinations.

Figure 1:

The search directions in existing methods (EG- and Past-EG-type) and our schemes (GEG-type): with
u^{k}:=\alpha_{1}Fx^{k}+\alpha_{2}Fy^{k-1}+\alpha_{3}v^{k-1}
for
\alpha_{1}=1
,
\alpha_{2}=0.5
, and
\alpha_{3}=-0.5
.

\bullet

Second, our generalized EAG method in Section
3
is new because it employs a general direction
u^{k}
as described above and accommodates
3
-cyclically monotone operators
T
(covering normal cone and subdifferential mappings as special cases, but not necessarily maximally monotone).
Our method subsumes several existing methods, including those in
[
18
,
79
,
87
]
, and can generate new variants by flexibly choosing
u^{k}
to satisfy the condition (
6
).
However, its applicability is currently limited to monotone
F
and
3
-cyclically monotone
T
, while extensions to non-monotone cases remain open.
Similarly, our generalized anchored FBFS method in Section
4
is novel and general, encompassing the algorithms from
[
19
,
40
,
77
]
as special cases.
It also allows one to generate new variants through flexible choices of
u^{k}
under the condition (
21
).
Nevertheless, the convergence of iterates is still unknown.

\bullet

Third, our moving-anchor FBFS method in Section
5
is more general and different from the one in
[
1
]
and a very recent work in
[
88
]
due to the anchor point update.
Note that the moving anchor trick is very similar to classical restarting techniques in optimization
[
59
]
and in fixed-point iterations
[
44
]
.
While
[
1
]
only achieves
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)
convergence rates under different settings,
[
88
]
was able to prove a sharper rate, i.e.
o\big(1/k\big)
, and the convergence of the iterates.
We achieve all of these convergence results for a much more general class of methods compared to
[
1
,
88
]
, covering new variants.

\bullet

Fourth, our generalized Nesterov’s accelerated method in Section
6
extends the first algorithm in
[
77
]
to a broader class of algorithms.
This includes novel variants, such as the case where
u^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}
.
Notably, when
u^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}
, our method covers those in
[
15
,
71
]
as special cases, while relaxing monotone assumption of
\Phi
to a co-hypomonotone one.
It also allows
T
to be a general (e.g., maximally monotone) mapping instead of a normal cone as in
[
71
]
.
However, we can only prove
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k\right)
rates of the residual norm
\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|
.

\bullet

Finally, our algorithm in Section
7
appears to be novel both in terms of its algorithmic form and its convergence analysis.
It differs from existing methods, even in special cases such as solving (
NE
) under monotonicity assumptions.
We establish both
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k\right)
and
o\left(1/k\right)
last-iterate convergence rates under co-hypomonotonicity of
\Phi
and Lipschitz continuity of
F
.
Our method subsumes those in
[
15
,
71
]
as special cases and allows for the creation of new variants by the selection of
u^{k}
.
Furthermore, our analysis is more intricate compared to the analyses in
[
15
,
71
]
and
[
88
]
due to the generalization of our scheme and the co-hypomonotonicity of
\Phi
.

1.3
Related work

Theory and numerical methods for (
NI
) and its special cases have been extensively studied for many decades, as evidenced in monographs such as
[
9
,
28
,
52
,
67
]
and the references therein.
In our recent work
[
81
]
, we studied several non-accelerated extragradient-type methods.
This paper focuses on both anchored (aka Halpern’s) and Nesterov’s accelerated counterparts of the extragradient method, which are expected to achieve faster convergence rates.
Therefore, we only review related work along this line.

\mathrm{(a)}
From non-accelerated to accelerated methods.

For the EG method and its variants employing constant stepsizes, as investigated in our recent work
[
81
]
, their convergence rate on the residual norm
\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|
for
\xi^{k}\in Tx^{k}
is
\mathcal{O}\big(1/\sqrt{k}\big)
.
This rate is unimprovable for constant parameters, as demonstrated in
[
30
]
.
To enhance the convergence rates of EG-type methods from
\mathcal{O}\big(1/\sqrt{k}\big)
to
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)
, it is essential to utilize variable parameters, including stepsizes, or [dual] averaging techniques
[
54
,
58
]
.
For variable parameters, there are at least two distinct approaches to developing accelerated methods for solving (
NE
) and (
NI
).
The first approach relies on Halpern’s fixed-point iteration
[
34
]
, while the second leverages Nesterov’s accelerated techniques.
Although these approaches were developed independently, they exhibit a connection as discussed in
[
78
]
.

\mathrm{(b)}
Halpern’s fixed-point iteration-type methods.

The Halpern fixed-point iteration, a classical method for approximating fixed points of nonexpansive operators (equivalently, finding roots of co-coercive operators), can be viewed as a blend of fixed-point iteration and adaptive Tikhonov regularization, as discussed in
[
14
]
.
Alternatively, it is also known as an anchored method revolves around a fixed initial point (i.e. anchor point).
While extensively studied in fixed-point theory, the first demonstration of its
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k\right)
last-iterate convergence rate was achieved by Sabach & Shtern in
[
70
]
and subsequently by Lieder in
[
42
]
.
Notably, this
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k\right)
rate on the residual norm is shown to be unimprovable in
[
42
]
through a constructive example.

In the last few years, we have seen significant advancements of Halpern’s fixed-point method, as evidenced by many works such as
[
26
,
40
,
79
,
87
]
.
Diakonikolas
et al.

[
26
]
effectively leveraged Halpern’s technique for solving both equations and variational inequalities.
A seminal contribution by Yoon and Ryu
[
87
]
extended Halpern’s iteration to the EG method for solving (
NE
), resulting in the “extra-anchored gradient” (EAG) method.
Remarkably, EAG maintains the
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k\right)
last-iterate convergence rate while only requiring monotonicity and Lipschitz continuity of
F
.
Building upon EAG, Lee and Kim
[
40
]
extended its applicability to the co-hypomonotone setting of (
NE
), achieving the same convergence rates.
Tran-Dinh and Luo
[
79
]
further expanded this line of research by applying the EAG framework to the past-extragradient method introduced by Popov
[
62
]
, leading to the “past extra-anchored gradient” (PEAG) method with the same
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k\right)
last-iterate convergence rates (up to a constant factor).
Recent works, such as
[
18
]
and
[
19
]
, have significantly extended these results in
[
40
,
79
,
87
]
to develop methods for solving (
VIP
) and (
NI
), all while preserving the desirable
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k\right)
last-iterate convergence rates for
\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|
.
Unlike Nesterov’s accelerated methods, both
o\big(1/k\big)
convergence rates and the convergence of iterates remain open in these works.

We suspect that Halpern-type methods suffer from a key limitation: parameter selection, typically
\tau_{k}=\frac{1}{k+\nu}
for some
\nu>1
(e.g.,
\nu=2
), restricts their flexibility and can potentially hinder their performance.
In addition, similar to original Nesterov’s accelerated methods, this choice of
\tau_{k}
may not allow us to prove faster convergence rates (e.g.,
o\left(1/k\right)
rates) as well as the convergence of iterates as discussed in
[
21
]
.
Moreover, the fixed anchor point contributes to all the iterates, potentially affects the overall performance.
Recent work by Yuan and Zhang
[
88
]
addresses these issues by adapting existing Halpern’s accelerated schemes
[
18
,
40
,
87
]
and the moving anchor EG method in
[
1
]
with a new parameter choice and a forward update for the anchor point as in
[
1
]
.
This approach, derived from a symplectic discretization of an associated ODE, achieves both
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k\right)
and
o\left(1/k\right)
last-iterate convergence rates on
\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|
under co-hypomonotonicity while enabling a more flexible parameter choice.
However, the focus of
[
88
]
remains on the extragradient method, leaving extensions to past-extragradient and other schemes as an open research question.
In this paper, we will also address this open question.

\mathrm{(c)}
Nesterov’s accelerated-type methods.

Alternatively, Nesterov’s accelerated method
[
55
]
stands as a significant breakthrough in convex optimization over the past few decades.
Though initially introduced in 1983, its widespread recognition surged with seminal works by Nesterov
[
57
]
and Beck and Teboulle
[
11
]
.
This powerful technique has since been extensively explored and applied across diverse fields, encompassing proximal-point, coordinate gradient, stochastic gradient, primal-dual, operator splitting, conditional gradient, Newton-type, and high-order methods.
While the majority of research on accelerated methods has focused on convex optimization, recent years have witnessed a surge of interest in extending these techniques to the realm of monotone equations and inclusions.
Early contributions in this direction include the works of Attouch et al.
[
3
,
6
]
, Bot
et al.

[
15
,
16
]
, Kim and Fessler
[
37
]
, and Maingé
[
46
,
47
]
, among many others.
In this paper, we go beyond the monotonicity in prior works to tackle a class of co-hypomonotone operators, possibly covering nonmonotone applications.

Developing accelerated methods for inclusions of the form (
NI
) encounters significant challenges compared to convex optimization, necessitating a fundamental shift in the construction of an appropriate Lyapunov function, serving as a key metric for convergence analysis.
Existing research often leverages proximal-point methods, which were extended to accelerated schemes in
[
32
]
.
An alternative approach to designing accelerated methods involves employing the “performance estimation problem” technique pioneered in
[
27
]
and further explored in
[
75
,
76
]
.
This technique has found successful applications in various works, including
[
33
,
37
,
69
]
.
Notably, Nesterov’s accelerated methods for diverse problem classes have been demonstrated to exhibit “optimal” convergence rates, meaning their upper bounds on convergence rates (or “oracle” complexity) align with the corresponding lower bounds in a specific sense, as evidenced in
[
56
,
86
]
, making them theoretically unimprovable.

\mathrm{(d)}
Other connections.

It has been demonstrated that Halpern’s and Nesterov’s accelerated methods can be interpreted as discretizations of corresponding dynamical systems, a perspective frequently observed in classical gradient methods
[
73
,
74
,
88
]
.
This viewpoint has spurred extensive research into new variants and extensions, as shown in works such as
[
5
,
15
,
16
,
72
,
85
]
.
Furthermore, connections between Nesterov’s method and other algorithms have been explored.
For instance,
[
4
]
establishes an equivalence between Nesterov’s accelerated methods and Ravine’s methods, originally proposed in 1961.
Recent studies, such as
[
60
,
78
]
, have revealed relationships between Nesterov’s accelerated schemes and Halpern’s fixed-point iterations
[
34
]
, demonstrating their equivalence under specific conditions.
Building upon this understanding,
[
77
,
78
]
have leveraged this perspective to develop novel Nesterov’s accelerated variants for solving (
NE
) and (
NI
), including extragradient methods.

1.4
Paper organization

Our paper is organized in such a way that each section from Section
3
to Section
7
presents one class of methods and its convergence theory and special instances.
Each section is relatively independent, and mostly does not depend on other sections.
It starts with the algorithm framework, special instances, key technical lemmas for convergence analysis, convergence theorems, and the convergence of special cases.
Technical lemmas show the flow of our analysis.
For clarity of presentation, we defer technical proofs to the appendix.

More specifically, the remainder of this paper is structured as follows.

1.

Section
2
provides a foundation by reviewing essential concepts and relevant prior results.
One important concept is the “co-hypomonotonicity”, see
[
10
]
.

2.

Section
3
introduces a novel class of anchored extragradient methods designed to address monotone inclusions (
NI
), along with their accompanying convergence guarantees and special instances.

3.

Building upon this, Section
4
generalizes and explores a class of anchored Tseng’s forward-backward-forward splitting schemes for solving (
NI
).
This section encompasses a unified convergence analysis of the proposed method, deriving convergence rates for specific instances.

4.

Section
5
proposes a class of moving anchor FBFS schemes for solving (
NI
) and establishes its convergence guarantees under the co-hypomonotonicity.

5.

Section
6
delves into a generalized class of Nesterov’s accelerated schemes and analyzes their convergence rates.

6.

Distinct from Sections
5
and
6
, Section
7
introduces a novel generalized Nesterov’s accelerated EG framework.
Notably, we establish both
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k\right)
and
o\left(1/k\right)
convergence rates for our method and its specific instances, while also demonstrating the convergence of iterates to a solution of (
NI
).

7.

Finally, Section
8
presents a set of numerical experiments to illustrate the theoretical aspects and practical ability of our proposed methods.

Although the technical proofs in this paper are both innovative and substantially distinct from prior works, we have elected to relocate all of them to the appendix. This decision was made primarily to enhance the overall presentation and readability of the main body of the paper.
The proofs are organized in each corresponding section as outlined in the main text of this paper.

2
Background and Preliminary Results

We recall several concepts which will be used in this paper.
These concepts and properties are well-known and can be found, e.g., in
[
9
,
28
,
64
,
65
,
68
]
.

2.1
Basic Concepts, Monotonicity, and Lipschitz Continuity

We work with finite dimensional Euclidean spaces
\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
equipped with standard inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and Euclidean norm
\|\cdot\|
.
For a multivalued mapping
T:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightrightarrows 2^{\mathbb{R}^{p}}
,
\mathrm{dom}(T)=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:Tx\not=\emptyset\right\}
denotes its domain,
\mathrm{ran}(T):=\bigcup_{x\in\mathrm{dom}(T)}Tx
is its range, and
\mathrm{gra}(T)=\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}:y\in Tx\right\}
stands for its graph, where
2^{\mathbb{R}^{p}}
is the set of all subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
The inverse mapping of
T
is defined as
T^{-1}y:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:y\in Tx\}
.
We say that
T
is
closed
if
\mathrm{gra}(T)
is closed.
For a proper, closed, and convex function
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
,
\mathrm{dom}(f):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:f(x)<+\infty\}
denotes the domain of
f
,
\partial{f}
denotes the subdifferential of
f
, and
\nabla{f}
stands for the [sub]gradient of
f
.

\mathrm{(a)}

Monotonicity and comonotonicity.

For a multivalued mapping
T:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightrightarrows 2^{\mathbb{R}^{p}}
and
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
, we say that
T
is
\rho
-comonotone (see
[
10
]
) if

\begin{array}[]{ll}\langle u-v,x-y\rangle\geq\rho\|u-v\|^{2},\quad\forall(x,u),(y,v)\in\mathrm{gra}(T).\end{array}

If
T
is single-valued, then this condition reduces to
\langle Tx-Ty,x-y\rangle\geq\rho\|Tx-Ty\|^{2}
for all
x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(T)
.

•

If
\rho=0
, then we say that
T
is monotone.

•

If
\rho>0
, then
T
is called
\rho
-co-coercive.
In particular, if
\rho=1
, then
T
is firmly nonexpansive.

•

If
\rho<0
, then
T
is called
|\rho|
-co-hypomonotone, see
[
10
,
23
]
.

Clearly,
T
is
|\rho|
-co-hypomonotone iff
T^{-1}
is
|\rho|
-hypomonotone, see
[
10
]
.
Note that a co-hyopomonotone operator can also be nonmonotone.
A simple example is
Tx=\mathbf{Q}x+\mathbf{q}
for a given invertible symmetric, but not necessarily positive definite matrix
\mathbf{Q}
, and
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
In this case, we have
\rho=-\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{Q}^{-1})>0
.

We say that
T
is maximally
\rho
-comonotone if
\mathrm{gra}(T)
is not properly contained in the graph of any other
\rho
-comonotone operator.
If
\rho=0
, then we say that
T
is maximally monotone.
Note that
T
is maximally monotone, then
\eta T
is also maximally monotone for any
\eta>0
, and if
T
and
F
are maximally monotone, and
\mathrm{dom}(T)\cap\mathrm{int}\left(\mathrm{dom}(F)\right)\not=\emptyset
, then
F+T
is maximally monotone.
For a proper, closed, and convex function
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
, the subdifferential
\partial{f}
of
f
is maximally monotone.

\mathrm{(b)}

Cyclic monotonicity.

We say that a mapping
T
is
m
-cyclically monotone (
m\geq 2
) if
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\langle u^{i},x^{i}-x^{i+1}\rangle\geq 0
for all
(x^{i},u^{i})\in\mathrm{gra}(T)
and
x_{1}=x_{m+1}
(see
[
9
]
).
We say that
T
is cyclically monotone if it is
m
-cyclically monotone for every
m\geq 2
.
If
T
is
m
-cyclically monotone, then it is also
\hat{m}
-cyclically monotone for any
2\leq\hat{m}\leq m
.
Since a
2
-cyclically monotone operator
T
is monotone, any
m
-cyclically monotone operator
T
is
2
-cyclically monotone, and thus is also monotone.
An
m
-cyclically monotone operator
T
is called maximally
m
-cyclically monotone if
\mathrm{gra}(T)
is not properly contained into the graph of any other
m
-cyclically monotone operator.

As proven in
(
9
, Theorem 22.18)
that
T
is maximally cyclically monotone iff
T=\partial{f}
, the subdifferential of a proper, closed, and convex function
f
.
On the one hand, there exist maximally monotone operators (e.g., rotation operators) that are not
3
-cyclically monotone, see
(
9
, Example 22.15)
.
On the other hand, as indicated in
(
8
, Example 2.16)
, there exist maximally
3
-cyclically monotone operators that are not maximally monotone.
These classes of operators are intersected with each other, and one is not a subclass of the other.

\mathrm{(c)}

Lipschitz continuity and contraction.

A multivalued mapping
T
is called
L
-Lipschitz continuous if
\sup\left\{\|u-v\|:u\in Tx,\ v\in Ty\right\}\leq L\|x-y\|
for all
x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(T)
, where
L\geq 0
is the Lipschitz constant.
If
T
is single-valued, then this condition reduces to
\|Tx-Ty\|\leq L\|x-y\|
for all
x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(T)
.
If
L=1
, then we say that
T
is nonexpansive, while if
L\in[0,1)
, then we say that
T
is
L
-contractive, and
L
is its contraction factor.
If
T
is
\rho
-co-coercive with
\rho>0
, then
T
is also
L
-Lipschitz continuous with the Lipschitz constant
L:=\frac{1}{\rho}
.
However, the reverse statement is not true in general.
For a continuously differentiable function
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}
, we say that
f
is
L
-smooth if its gradient
\nabla{f}
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous on
\mathrm{dom}(f)
.
If
f
is convex and
L
-smooth, then
\nabla{f}
is
\frac{1}{L}
-co-coercive and vice versa, see, e.g.,
[
56
]
.

\mathrm{(d)}

Normal cone.

Given a nonempty, closed, and convex set
\mathcal{X}
in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
, the normal cone of
\mathcal{X}
is defined as
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}}(x):=\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\langle w,x-y\rangle\geq 0,\ \forall y\in\mathcal{X}\}
if
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}}(x)=\emptyset
, otherwise.
If
f:=\delta_{\mathcal{X}}
, the indicator of a convex set
\mathcal{X}
, then we have
\partial{f}=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}}
.
Moreover,
J_{\partial{f}}
reduces to the projection onto
\mathcal{X}
.

\mathrm{(e)}

Resolvent and proximal operators.

Given a multivalued operator
T
, the operator
J_{T}x:=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:x\in y+Ty\right\}
is called the resolvent of
T
, denoted by
J_{T}x=(\mathbb{I}+T)^{-1}x
, where
\mathbb{I}
is the identity mapping.
If
T
is monotone, then evaluating
J_{T}
requires solving a strongly monotone inclusion
0\in y-x+Ty
.
Hence,
J_{T}
is well-defined and single-valued.
If
T=\partial{f}
, the subdifferential of proper, closed, and convex function
f
, then
J_{T}
reduces to the proximal operator of
f
, denoted by
\mathrm{prox}_{f}
, which can be computed as
\mathrm{prox}_{f}(x):=\mathrm{arg}\min_{y}\{f(y)+(1/2)\|y-x\|^{2}\}
.
In particular, if
T=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}}
, the normal cone of a closed and convex set
\mathcal{X}
, then
J_{T}
is the projection onto
\mathcal{X}
, denoted by
\mathrm{proj}_{\mathcal{X}}
.
If
T
is maximally monotone, then
\mathrm{ran}(\mathbb{I}+T)=\mathbb{R}^{p}
(by Minty’s theorem) and
T
is firmly nonexpansive (and thus nonexpansive).

2.2
Exact Solutions and Approximate Solutions

There are different metrics to characterize exact and approximate solutions of (
NI
).
The most obvious one is the residual norm of
\Phi
, which is defined as

r(x):=\min_{\xi\in Tx}\|Fx+\xi\|,\quad x\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi).\vskip-2.15277pt

(2)

Clearly,
r(x^{\star})=0
iff
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
, a solution of (
NI
).
If
T=0
, then
r(x)=\|Fx\|
.
Given a tolerance
\epsilon>0
, if
r(\hat{x})\leq\epsilon
, then we say that
\hat{x}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate solution to (
NI
).
The algorithms presented in this paper use this metric as the main tool to characterize approximate solutions to (
NI
).

Other metrics often used for monotone (
VIP
), a special case of (
NI
), are gap and restricted gap functions
[
28
,
38
,
58
]
, which are respectively defined as

\mathbb{G}(x):=\max_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\langle Fy,y-x\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{B}}(x):=\max_{y\in\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{B}}\langle Fy,y-x\rangle,\vskip-2.15277pt

(3)

where
\mathcal{B}
is a given nonempty, closed, and bounded convex set.
Note that, under the monotonicity of
F
,
\mathbb{G}(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and
\mathbb{G}(x^{\star})=0
iff
x^{\star}
is a solution to (
VIP
).
Therefore, to characterize an
\epsilon
-approximate solution
\tilde{x}
to (
VIP
), we can impose a condition
\mathbb{G}(\tilde{x})\leq\epsilon
.

For the restricted gap function
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{B}}
, if
x^{\star}
is a solution of (
VIP
) and
x^{\star}\in\mathcal{B}
, then
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{B}}(x^{\star})=0
.
Conversely, if
\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{B}}(x^{\star})=0
and
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{int}\left(\mathcal{B}\right)
, the interior of
\mathcal{B}
, then
x^{\star}
is a solution of (
VIP
) in
\mathcal{B}
(see
(
58
, Lemma 1)
).
For (
DVIP
), we can also define similar dual gap functions and restricted dual gap functions
[
58
]
.
Gap functions have widely been used in the literature to characterize approximate solutions generated by many numerical methods for solving monotone (
VIP
) or (
DVIP
), see, e.g.,
[
22
,
24
,
28
,
38
,
54
,
58
]
as concrete examples.

If
J_{\eta T}
is well-defined and single-valued for some
\eta>0
, and
F
is single-valued, then we can use the following forward-backward splitting residual:

\mathcal{G}_{\eta}(x):=\tfrac{1}{\eta}\left(x-J_{\eta T}(x-\eta Fx)\right),\vskip-2.15277pt

(4)

to characterize solutions of (
NI
), where
F
is single-valued and
J_{\eta T}
is the resolvent of
\eta T
for any
\eta>0
.
It is clear that
\mathcal{G}_{\eta}(x^{\star})=0
iff
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
.
In addition, if
J_{\eta T}
is firmly nonexpansive, then we also have

\|\mathcal{G}_{\eta}(x)\|\leq\|Fx+\xi\|,\quad(x,\xi)\in\mathrm{gra}(T).

(5)

Hence, for a given tolerance
\epsilon>0
, if
\|\mathcal{G}_{\eta}(\tilde{x})\|\leq\epsilon
, then we can say that
\tilde{x}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate solution of (
NI
).
If
T:=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}}
, i.e. (
NI
) reduces to (
VIP
), then
\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
reduces to the classical natural map
\Pi_{F,\mathcal{X}}(x)=x-\mathrm{proj}_{\mathcal{X}}(x-Fx)
of (
VIP
), and
r_{n}(x):=\|\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)\|=\|\Pi_{F,\mathcal{X}}(x)\|
is the corresponding natural residual at
x
.
From (
5
), we have
r_{n}(x)\leq\|Fx+\xi\|
for any
\xi\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}}(x)
.
The natural residual is a fundamental metric to study generalized equations of the form (
NI
) and (
VIP
), see, e.g.,
[
28
]
for more details.

Remark 1

Note that characterizing an [approximate] solution via gap or restricted gap function for (
VIP
) requires the monotonicity of
F
to upper bound
\mathbb{G}(x^{k})
by
\mathbb{G}(x^{k})\leq\max_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\langle Fx^{k},x^{k}-y\rangle
.
For the nonmonotone case, including co-hypomonotonicity, these gap functions are not applicable in general.

3
A Class of Extra-Anchored Gradient Methods for Monotone (
NI
)

In this section, we generalize the extra-anchored gradient method (EAG) in
[
87
]
for (
NE
) and in
[
18
]
for (
VIP
) to a more general class of algorithms and for monotone inclusions of the form (
NI
).
We provide a unified convergence rate analysis for our scheme and derive special instances.

3.1
A Class of Extra-Anchored Gradient Methods for (
NI
)

\mathrm{(a)}

The proposed method.

We propose the following scheme to solve (
NI
).
Starting from
x^{0}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)
, at each iteration
k\geq 0
, given
u^{k}
, we update

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}y^{k}&:=&J_{\hat{\eta}_{k}T}\left(\tau_{k}x^{0}+(1-\tau_{k})x^{k}-\hat{\eta}_{k}u^{k}\right),\\
x^{k+1}&:=&J_{\eta T}\left(\tau_{k}x^{0}+(1-\tau_{k})x^{k}-\eta Fy^{k}\right),\end{array}\right.

(GEAG)

where
\tau_{k}\in(0,1)
,
\hat{\eta}_{k}>0
, and
\eta>0
are given and will be determined later, and
u^{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is a user-defined direction satisfying the following condition:

\|u^{k}\!-\!Fx^{k}\|^{2}\leq\kappa\|Fx^{k}\!-\!Fy^{k-1}\|^{2}+\hat{\kappa}\|x^{k}\!-\!y^{k-1}\!+\!\hat{\eta}_{k-1}(Fx^{k-1}\!-\!u^{k-1})\|^{2},

(6)

for given parameters
\kappa\geq 0
and
\hat{\kappa}\geq 0
,
y^{-1}:=x^{0}
, and
u^{-1}=u^{0}:=Fx^{0}
.
This condition looks slightly technical, but we will explain later how it is constructed.
Our generalization in (
GEAG
) consists of the following new points:

\mathrm{(i)}

We assume that
T
is maximally
3
-cyclically monotone.
This choice covers
T=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}}
, the normal cone of
\mathcal{X}
, and
T=\partial{g}
, the subdifferential of a convex function
g
as special cases, but it is more general than the subdifferentials, and is not identical to the class of maximally monotone operators.

\mathrm{(ii)}

We use a user-defined direction
u^{k}
that satisfies (
6
), which covers
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
and
u^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}
as special instances.

\mathrm{(b)}

Three instances.

By different choices of
u^{k}
, (
GEAG
) covers both existing and new variants of the extra-anchored gradient (EAG) methods for solving monotone inclusions of the form (
NI
).
Here, let us consider at least three instances of (
GEAG
) as follows.

\mathrm{(i)}

Variant 1 (EAG).
If we choose
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
, then (
6
) holds with
\kappa=0
and
\hat{\kappa}=0
.
Clearly, this variant of (
GEAG
) covers the method in
[
18
]
as a special case when
T=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}}
.
If, in addition,
T=0
, then this variant reduces to the extra-anchored gradient (EAG) scheme from
[
87
]
.
In fact,
GEAG
purely generalizes
[
87
]
from (
NE
) to (
NI
) with a general extra-gradient direction
u^{k}
, determined appropriately, and a
3
-cyclically monotone
T
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

Variant 2 (Popov’s past EAG).
If
u^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}
, then (
6
) holds with
\kappa=1
and
\hat{\kappa}=0
.
This variant of (
GEAG
) covers the past-extra-anchored gradient method in
[
79
]
as a special case with
T:=0
.
However, this variant has not yet been studied for (
VIP
) and a general monotone inclusion (
NI
).

\mathrm{(iii)}

Variant 3 (Generalized EAG).
If we choose

u^{k}:=(1-\alpha)Fx^{k}+\alpha Fy^{k-1}+\hat{\alpha}\big(x^{k}-y^{k-1}+\hat{\eta}_{k-1}(Fx^{k-1}-u^{k-1})\big),

(7)

for any
\alpha,\hat{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
, then
u^{k}
satisfies (
6
) with
\kappa:=\alpha^{2}\geq 0
and
\hat{\kappa}:=\hat{\alpha}^{2}\geq 0
.
Clearly, this choice takes the advantages of both
Variant 1
and
Variant 2
, while still covers other possibilities generated by the term
v^{k-1}:=x^{k}-y^{k-1}+\hat{\eta}_{k-1}(Fx^{k-1}-u^{k-1})
.
In particular, if
T=0
, then it is trivial to show that the term
v^{k}
reduces to
v^{k-1}:=\hat{\eta}_{k-1}Fx^{k-1}-\eta Fy^{k-1}
.
In this case, we get
u^{k}=(1-\alpha)Fx^{k}+(\alpha-\hat{\alpha}\eta)Fy^{k-1}+\hat{\alpha}\hat{\eta}_{k-1}Fx^{k-1}
, which is a linear combination of
Fx^{k}
,
Fy^{k-1}
, and
Fx^{k-1}
.

Note that the per-iteration cost of
Variant 1
and
Variant 3
is essentially the same with two evaluations
Fx^{k}
and
Fy^{k}
of
F
, while
Variant 2
only requires one evaluation
Fy^{k}
of
F
.
Note also that, due to the flexibility of choosing
\alpha
and also
\hat{\alpha}
,
Variant 3
covers
Variant 1
and
Variant 2
as special cases.

If
T=0
, then our methods presented above can be applied to solve (
NE
).
If
T=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}}
, the normal cone of a convex set
\mathcal{X}
, then they can be utilized to solve (
VIP
).
If
T:=\partial{g}
, the subdifferential of a convex function
g
, then our methods reduce to the variants for solving (
MVIP
).
Obviously, our methods can also be specified to solve (
FixP
) and (
1
).
However, we omit the details of these applications and leave them open to the readers.

3.2
Key Estimates for Convergence Analysis

To facilitate the form of condition (
6
) and to analyze the convergence of (
GEAG
), we define the following quantities:

\begin{array}[]{llcl}&w^{k}:=Fx^{k}+\xi^{k},&&\quad\hat{w}^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}+\xi^{k},\\
&\tilde{w}^{k}:=Fx^{k}+\zeta^{k},&\quad\textrm{and}&\quad\tilde{z}^{k}:=u^{k}+\zeta^{k},\end{array}

(8)

for some
\xi^{k}\in Tx^{k}
and
\zeta^{k}\in Ty^{k}
.
Then, we can easily see that
w^{k}-\hat{w}^{k}=Fx^{k}-Fy^{k-1}
and
\tilde{w}^{k}-\tilde{z}^{k}=Fx^{k}-u^{k}
.

\mathrm{(a)}

Equivalent form.

Using these quantities and the fact that
w=J_{\eta T}(v)
iff
v-w\in\eta Tw
, we can equivalently rewrite (
GEAG
) as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}y^{k}&:=&\tau_{k}x^{0}+(1-\tau_{k})x^{k}-\hat{\eta}_{k}\tilde{z}^{k},\\
x^{k+1}&:=&\tau_{k}x^{0}+(1-\tau_{k})x^{k}-\eta\hat{w}^{k+1}.\end{array}\right.

(9)

Moreover, by (
8
), (
9
), and
\hat{\eta}_{k-1}:=\eta(1-\tau_{k-1})
from (
15
) below, we get

\begin{array}[]{lcl}x^{k}-y^{k-1}+\hat{\eta}_{k-1}(Fx^{k-1}-u^{k-1})&\overset{\tiny\eqref{eq:EAG4NI_ex2},\eqref{eq:EAG4NI_reform}}{=}&-\eta\hat{w}^{k}+\hat{\eta}_{k-1}\tilde{z}^{k-1}+\hat{\eta}_{k-1}(\tilde{w}^{k-1}-\tilde{z}^{k-1})\\
&=&-\eta[\hat{w}^{k}-(1-\tau_{k-1})\tilde{w}^{k-1}].\end{array}

Therefore, the condition (
6
) is equivalent to the following form:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\|u^{k}-Fx^{k}\|^{2}\leq\kappa\|Fx^{k}-Fy^{k-1}\|^{2}+\hat{\kappa}\eta^{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}-(1-\tau_{k-1})\tilde{w}^{k-1}\|^{2}.\end{array}

(10)

This condition will be used in our analysis below, and it also explains how the condition (
6
) is constructed.

\mathrm{(b)}

Lyapunov function.

To establish the convergence of (
GEAG
), we define the following functions:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{V}_{k}&:=&\frac{a_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+b_{k}\langle w^{k},x^{k}-x^{0}\rangle+\frac{b_{0}}{\eta}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2},\\
\mathcal{L}_{k}&:=&\mathcal{V}_{k}+\frac{c_{k}}{2}\|u^{k}-Fx^{k}\|^{2},\end{array}

(11)

where
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
,
a_{k}
,
b_{k}
and
c_{k}
are nonnegative parameters, determined later.
Note that
\mathcal{V}_{k}
has been widely used in the literature, including
[
18
,
40
,
77
,
87
]
as a Lyapunov function to analyze convergence of extra-anchored gradient-type methods.
Since we study the generalized scheme (
GEAG
), we need to add one more term
\frac{c_{k}}{2}\|u^{k}-Fx^{k}\|^{2}
to
\mathcal{V}_{k}
to handle the difference between
u^{k}
and
Fx^{k}
.

\mathrm{(c)}

Descent property.

Now, we state the following descent property of
\mathcal{V}_{k}
.

Lemma 1

For (
NI
), assume that
\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)\neq\emptyset
,
F
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous and monotone, and
T
is maximally
3
-cyclically monotone.
Let
\{(x^{k},y^{k})\}
be generated by (
GEAG
) satisfying (
6
) and
\mathcal{V}_{k}
be defined by (
11
).
Suppose that

\begin{array}[]{ll}\tau_{k}\in(0,1),\quad\hat{\eta}_{k}:=\eta(1-\tau_{k}),\quad a_{k}:=\frac{\eta b_{k}(1-\tau_{k})}{\tau_{k}},\quad\text{and}\quad b_{k+1}:=\frac{b_{k}}{1-\tau_{k}}.\end{array}

(12)

Then, for any
\omega>0
,
r>0
, and
c>0
, let
M_{c}:=(1+c)(1+\omega)L^{2}
, we have

\hskip-4.30554pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{V}_{k}-\mathcal{V}_{k+1}&\geq&\frac{\eta b_{k+1}[\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}(1-\tau_{k+1})]}{2\tau_{k}\tau_{k+1}}\|w^{k+1}\|^{2}+\frac{\omega\eta b_{k+1}}{2\tau_{k}}\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}\frac{(1-r)a_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}-\tilde{w}^{k}\|^{2}-\frac{(c+rM_{c}\eta^{2})a_{k}}{2rc}\|Fx^{k}-u^{k}\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}\frac{\eta b_{k+1}(1-M_{c}\eta^{2})}{2\tau_{k}}\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-(1-\tau_{k})\tilde{w}^{k}\|^{2}.\end{array}\hskip-12.91663pt

(13)

3.3
Convergence Analysis of (
GEAG
) and Its Special Instances

\mathrm{(a)}

The convergence of (
GEAG
).

For
\kappa
and
\hat{\kappa}
from (
6
), let
r\in(0,1]
and

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bar{\eta}:=\frac{\sqrt{r}}{\sqrt{(1+r)(r+2\kappa)L^{2}+2\kappa\hat{\kappa}}}.\end{array}

(14)

Now, we are ready to state the convergence of (
GEAG
).

Theorem 3.1

For (
NI
), assume that
\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)\neq\emptyset
,
F
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous and monotone, and
T
is maximally
3
-cyclically monotone.
Let
\{(x^{k},y^{k})\}
be generated by (
GEAG
) such that
u^{k}
satisfies (
6
) and

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\tau_{k}:=\frac{1}{k+\nu},\quad\eta_{k}:=\eta\in\left(0,\bar{\eta}\right],\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\eta}_{k}:=\eta(1-\tau_{k}),\end{array}

(15)

where
\nu>1
is given and
\bar{\eta}
is given in (
14
).
Then, for any
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
and
\xi^{k}\in Tx^{k}
, the following result holds:

\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}+\eta^{2}\|Fx^{0}+\xi^{0}\|^{2}}{\eta^{2}(k+\nu-1)^{2}}.

(16)

\mathrm{(b)}

Two special instances.

Next, we derive the convergence of the two special instances of (
GEAG
): the extra-anchored gradient (EAG) method with
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
(
Variant 1
), and the past-extra-anchored gradient (PEAG) method with
u^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}
(
Variant 2
).

Corollary 1

For (
NI
), assume that
\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)\neq\emptyset
,
F
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous and monotone, and
T
is maximally
3
-cyclically monotone.
Let
\{(x^{k},y^{k})\}
be generated by (
GEAG
) using either
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
or
u^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}
.

\mathrm{(i)}

Variant 1.

We choose
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
and the parameters as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\tau_{k}:=\frac{1}{k+\nu},\quad\eta_{k}:=\eta\in\big(0,\frac{1}{L}\big],\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\eta}_{k}:=\eta(1-\tau_{k}),\quad(\nu>1).\end{array}

(17)

\mathrm{(ii)}

Variant 2.

We choose
u^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}
and the parameters as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\tau_{k}:=\frac{1}{k+\nu},\quad\eta_{k}:=\eta\in\big(0,\frac{1}{L\sqrt{6}}\big],\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\eta}_{k}:=\eta(1-\tau_{k}).\end{array}

(18)

Then, for
\xi^{k}\in Tx^{k}
and
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
, the following result holds:

\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}+\eta^{2}\|Fx^{0}+\xi^{0}\|^{2}}{\eta^{2}(k+\nu-1)^{2}}.

(19)

Proof

\mathrm{(i)}
If
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
, then (
6
) holds with
\kappa=\hat{\kappa}=0
.
From (
14
), we have
\bar{\eta}:=\frac{1}{L\sqrt{1+r}}
.
Letting
r\downarrow 0^{+}
, the condition
\eta\in(0,\bar{\eta}]
in Theorem
3.1
becomes
\eta\in\big(0,\frac{1}{L}\big]
as in (
17
).

\mathrm{(ii)}
If
u^{k}=Fy^{k-1}
, then (
6
) holds with
\kappa=1
and
\hat{\kappa}=0
.
In this case, from (
14
), we have
\bar{\eta}:=\frac{\sqrt{r}}{L\sqrt{(1+r)(2+r)}}
.
If we choose
r:=1
, then we get
\bar{\eta}:=\frac{1}{L\sqrt{6}}
, leading to the choice of
\eta
as in (
18
).

In both cases, we can conclude that the bound (
19
) is a direct consequence of (
16
) from Theorem
3.1
.

\square

4
A Class of Anchored FBFS Methods for Inclusion (
NI
)

In this section, we develop another class of Halpern-type methods by generalizing the fast extragradient method in
[
40
]
to solve (
NI
).
Note that this method is different from (
GEAG
) as it is rooted from Tseng’s forward-backward-forward splitting (FBFS) method
[
83
]
instead of the extragradient method from
[
39
]
.
In addition, we relax our assumption from monotonicity to co-hypomonotonicity.

4.1
A Class of Anchored FBFS Methods

First, for given
x^{k}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)
and
u^{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we define the following elements:

w^{k}:=Fx^{k}+\xi^{k},\quad\hat{w}^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}+\xi^{k},\quad\text{and}\quad z^{k}:=u^{k}+\xi^{k},

(20)

for some
\xi^{k}\in Tx^{k}
.

\mathrm{(a)}

The proposed method.

Our algorithm is described as follows.
Starting from
x^{0}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)
, at each iteration
k\geq 0
, for given
(x^{k},u^{k})
, we update

\hskip-12.91663pt\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}y^{k}&:=&x^{k}+\tau_{k}(x^{0}-x^{k})-(\hat{\eta}_{k}-\beta_{k})z^{k},\\
x^{k+1}&:=&x^{k}+\tau_{k}(x^{0}-x^{k})-\eta\hat{w}^{k+1}+\beta_{k}z^{k},\end{array}\right.\hskip-12.91663pt

(GFEG)

where
\tau_{k}\in(0,1)
,
\eta>0
,
\hat{\eta}_{k}>0
, and
\beta_{k}\geq 0
are given, determined later.
The term
u^{k}
in
z^{k}
is chosen such that it satisfies the following condition:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\|Fx^{k}-u^{k}\|^{2}&\leq&\kappa\|Fx^{k}-Fy^{k-1}\|^{2}+\hat{\kappa}\|w^{k}-w^{k-1}\|^{2},\end{array}

(21)

for given constants
\kappa\geq 0
and
\hat{\kappa}\geq 0
,
y^{-1}=x^{-1}:=x^{0}
, and
u^{0}:=Fx^{0}
.

\mathrm{(b)}

Three special instances.

We consider three special instances of (
GFEG
).

\mathrm{(i)}

Variant 1: The anchored FBFS method.
If we choose
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
, then (
21
) holds with
\kappa=0
and
\hat{\kappa}=0
.
Moreover, we can see that (
GFEG
) covers the following existing methods as special cases.

•

If
T=0
,
\beta_{k}:=0
, and
\hat{\eta}_{k}=\eta=\eta_{k}
(fixed or varying), then (
GFEG
) reduces to the extra-anchored gradient (EAG) scheme for solving (
NE
) in
[
87
]
under the monotonicity of
F
as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}y^{k}&:=&\tau_{k}x^{0}+(1-\tau_{k})x^{k}-\eta_{k}Fx^{k},\\
x^{k+1}&=&\tau_{k}x^{0}+(1-\tau_{k})x^{k}-\eta_{k}Fy^{k}.\end{array}\right.

•

If
T=0
,
\beta_{k}:=2\rho(1-\tau_{k})
, and
\hat{\eta}_{k}:=\eta(1-\tau_{k})
, then (
GFEG
) reduces to the fast extragradient (FEG) variant for solving (
NE
) in
[
40
]
, but under the co-hypomonotonicity of
F
.

•

If
T
is a maximally monotone operator (e.g.,
T:=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}}
, the normal cone of a nonempty, closed, and convex set
\mathcal{X}
),
\beta_{k}:=2\rho(1-\tau_{k})
and
\hat{\eta}_{k}:=\eta(1-\tau_{k})
, then (
GFEG
) is exactly the variant of EAG studied in
[
18
]
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

Variant 2: Anchored FRBS method.

If we choose
u^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}
, then (
21
) holds with
\kappa=1
and
\hat{\kappa}=0
.
In this case, (
GFEG
) reduces to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}y^{k}&:=&\tau_{k}x^{0}+(1-\tau_{k})x^{k}-(\hat{\eta}_{k}-\beta_{k})\hat{w}^{k}\\
x^{k+1}&:=&\tau_{k}x^{0}+(1-\tau_{k})x^{k}-\eta\hat{w}^{k+1}+\beta_{k}\hat{w}^{k}.\end{array}\right.

(22)

This scheme is a variant of
Popov’s past extra-gradient
method in
[
62
]
.
We call it the
anchored FRBS
scheme that covers variants studied in
[
19
,
77
]
.
This scheme can also be viewed as a Halpern’s accelerated variant of the
forward-reflected backward splitting (FRBS)
or the
optimistic gradient
method in the literature, see also
[
25
,
51
,
53
]
.

When
T=0
, as discussed in Part 1 of our work
[
81
]
, we can view (
GFEG
) as a Halpern’s acceleration of the
forward-reflected-backward splitting
scheme in
[
50
]
, which is also equivalent to the
reflected gradient
method in
[
48
]
, the
reflected forward-backward splitting
scheme in
[
20
]
, and the
golden ratio
method in
[
49
]
for solving (
NE
).
This scheme can also be viewed as a Halpern’s accelerated variant of the
optimistic gradient
method in the literature, see also
[
25
,
51
,
53
]
.
Hence, (
GFEG
) is sufficiently general to cover several existing methods as its instances.

\mathrm{(iii)}

Variant 3: Generalization.

We can construct a generalized direction:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}u^{k}&:=&\alpha Fx^{k}+\hat{\alpha}Fy^{k-1}+(1-\alpha-\hat{\alpha})Fx^{k-1}+\hat{\alpha}(\xi^{k}-\xi^{k-1}),\end{array}

(23)

for any given constants
\alpha,\hat{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Clearly,
u^{k}
is an affine combination of
Fx^{k}
,
Fy^{k-1}
,
Fx^{k-1}
, and
\xi^{k}-\xi^{k-1}
.
Moreover, we can verify that
u^{k}
satisfies (
21
) with
\kappa=(1+m)\hat{\alpha}^{2}
and
\hat{\kappa}=(1+m^{-1})(1-\alpha-\hat{\alpha})^{2}
by Young’s inequality for any
m>0
.
Generally,
u^{k}
requires
Fx^{k}
and/or
Fx^{k-1}
, and thus (
GFEG
) needs at most two evaluations of
F
at each iteration
k
, which is the same cost as
Variant 1
.

In fact, (
GFEG
) is rooted from Tseng’s forward-backward-forward splitting method
[
83
]
instead of the extragradient method
[
39
]
because it only requires one resolvent evaluation
J_{\eta T}
per iteration.
Recently,
[
77
]
provides an elementary convergence analysis for (
GFEG
), which relies on the technique in
[
87
]
.
In this paper, we generalize these variants to (
GFEG
), which covers a wide range of variants, including
[
40
,
77
,
87
]
as special instances.

In particular, if
T=0
, then (
GFEG
) solves (
NE
), and (
21
) reduces to

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\|Fx^{k}-u^{k}\|^{2}&\leq&\kappa\|Fx^{k}-Fy^{k-1}\|^{2}+\hat{\kappa}\|Fx^{k}-Fx^{k-1}\|^{2}.\end{array}

In addition,
u^{k}
in (
23
) reduces to
u^{k}:=\alpha Fx^{k}+\hat{\alpha}Fy^{k-1}+(1-\alpha-\hat{\alpha})Fx^{k-1}
.

\mathrm{(c)}

The implementation of (
GFEG
)
.

Since
x^{k+1}
are in both sides of line 2 of (
GFEG
), by using the resolvent
J_{\eta T}
of
T
, we can rewrite (
GFEG
) as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}y^{k}&:=&x^{k}+\tau_{k}(x^{0}-x^{k})-(\hat{\eta}_{k}-\beta_{k})(u^{k}+\xi^{k}),\\
x^{k+1}&:=&J_{\eta T}\left(y^{k}-\eta Fy^{k}+\hat{\eta}_{k}(u^{k}+\xi^{k})\right),\\
\xi^{k+1}&:=&\frac{1}{\eta}\big[y^{k}+\hat{\eta}_{k}(u^{k}+\xi^{k})-x^{k+1}\big]-Fy^{k},\end{array}\right.

(24)

where
w^{k}:=Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}
,
\xi^{0}\in Tx^{0}
is arbitrary.

Generally, we do not require
J_{\eta T}
to be single-valued, but only assume that
\mathrm{ran}(J_{\eta T})\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(F)=\mathbb{R}^{p}
, and
\mathrm{dom}(J_{\eta T})=\mathbb{R}^{p}
so that the iterates are well-defined.
However, to simplify our analysis, we assume that
J_{\eta T}
is single-valued.
In this case, we say that
J_{\eta T}
is
well-defined
.

Since
w^{k}:=Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}
, the condition (
21
) can be rewritten as

\|Fx^{k}-u^{k}\|^{2}\leq\kappa\|Fx^{k}-Fy^{k-1}\|^{2}+\hat{\kappa}\|Fx^{k}-Fx^{k-1}+\xi^{k}-\xi^{k-1}\|^{2}.

We have provided three concrete choices of
u^{k}
as discussed above, but any direction
u^{k}
satisfying this condition still works.

Remark 2

In fact, we can relax our condition (
21
) to the following one:

\|Fx^{k}-u^{k}\|^{2}\leq\kappa\|Fx^{k}-Fy^{k-1}\|^{2}+\hat{\kappa}\|w^{k}-w^{k-1}\|^{2}+\bar{\kappa}\|\hat{w}^{k}-(1-\tau_{k-1})w^{k-1}\|^{2},

for given constants
\kappa,\hat{\kappa},\bar{\kappa}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Then, our analysis bellow still goes through.
Nevertheless, analyzing this case is relatively involved as we need to process an additional parameter
\bar{\kappa}
.
Hence, we do not include it in this paper.

4.2
Key Estimates for Convergence Analysis

\mathrm{(a)}

Lyapunov function.

Similar to Section
3
, we will use the following Lyapunov function to analyze the convergence of (
GFEG
):

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{L}_{k}&:=&\frac{a_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+b_{k}\langle w^{k},x^{k}-x^{0}\rangle+\frac{c_{k}}{2}\|u^{k}-Fx^{k}\|^{2},\end{array}

(25)

where
a_{k}
,
b_{k}
, and
c_{k}
are given nonnegative parameters, determined later.
Compared to
\mathcal{L}_{k}
in (
11
) of Section
3
, we drop the last term
\frac{b_{0}}{\eta}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}
here.

\mathrm{(b)}

Key lemma.

The following lemma provides a key step to analyze the convergence of (
GFEG
).

Lemma 2

For (
NI
), suppose that
F
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous,
J_{\eta T}
is well-defined, and
\Phi
is
\rho
-co-hypomonotone.
Let
\{(x^{k},y^{k})\}
be generated by (
GFEG
) starting from
x^{0}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)
such that
u^{k}
satisfies (
21
).
For a fixed
\eta>0
and a given
b_{k}>0
, suppose that

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\tau_{k}\in(0,1),\quad\eta_{k}:=\eta(1-\tau_{k}),\quad\text{and}\quad b_{k+1}:=\frac{b_{k}}{1-\tau_{k}}.\end{array}

(26)

For any
\mu\in[0,1]
,
r>0
, and
\gamma>0
, let us denote

\hskip-12.91663pt\left\{\begin{array}[]{lclllcl}a_{k+1}&:=&\frac{b_{k+1}[\eta-\beta_{k}(1+\gamma\tau_{k})]}{\tau_{k}},&&\hat{a}_{k}&:=&\frac{b_{k}[\eta(1-\tau_{k})^{2}-\beta_{k}(1-2\tau_{k}+\gamma\tau_{k}(1-\tau_{k}))]}{\tau_{k}(1-\tau_{k})},\\
\alpha_{k+1}&:=&\frac{(1-\mu)\eta b_{k+1}}{\mu\tau_{k}},&&\hat{\alpha}_{k+1}&:=&\frac{b_{k+1}[\beta_{k}(1-\gamma+\gamma\tau_{k})-2\rho(1-\tau_{k})]}{\tau_{k}},\\
\delta_{k+1}&:=&\frac{\eta b_{k+1}[\mu-(1+r)L^{2}\eta^{2}]}{\mu\tau_{k}}&\quad\text{and}&c_{k}&:=&\frac{b_{k}[(1+r)L^{2}\eta^{3}\gamma(1-\tau_{k})^{2}+r\mu\beta_{k}]}{r\mu\gamma\tau_{k}(1-\tau_{k})}.\end{array}\right.\hskip-25.83325pt

(27)

Then the function

\hskip-8.61108pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{P}_{k}&:=&\frac{\hat{a}_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+b_{k}\langle w^{k},x^{k}-x^{0}\rangle+\frac{c_{k}}{2}\|Fx^{k}-u^{k}\|^{2}\end{array}\hskip-8.61108pt

(28)

satisfies the following property:

\hskip-8.61108pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{P}_{k}&\geq&\frac{a_{k+1}}{2}\|w^{k+1}\|^{2}+b_{k+1}\langle w^{k+1},x^{k+1}-x^{0}\rangle+\frac{\alpha_{k+1}}{2}\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}\frac{\hat{\alpha}_{k+1}}{2}\|w^{k+1}-w^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{\delta_{k+1}}{2}\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-(1-\tau_{k})w^{k}\|^{2}.\end{array}\hskip-8.61108pt

(29)

\mathrm{(c)}

Descent property.

Next, we establish a descent property of
\mathcal{L}_{k}
in (
25
).

Lemma 3

Under the same settings as in Lemma
2
, assume that
a_{k}
,
\hat{a}_{k}
,
b_{k}
,
\alpha_{k}
,
\hat{\alpha}_{k}
,
c_{k}
, and
\delta_{k}
given in (
27
) satisfy:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}a_{k}\geq\hat{a}_{k}\geq 0,\quad\alpha_{k}\geq\kappa c_{k},\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\alpha}_{k}\geq\hat{\kappa}c_{k}.\end{array}

(30)

Then,
\mathcal{L}_{k}
defined by (
25
) satisfies the following inequality:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{L}_{k}\geq\mathcal{L}_{k+1}+\frac{\delta_{k+1}}{2}\|\hat{w}^{k+1}-(1-\tau_{k})w^{k}\|^{2}.\end{array}

(31)

Lemma 4

Under the same settings as in Lemma
3
, suppose that for a fixed
\nu>2
,
\tau_{k}
and
\beta_{k}
are chosen as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\tau_{k}:=\frac{1}{k+\nu}\quad\text{and}\quad{\beta_{k}:=\beta(1-\tau_{k})},\end{array}

(32)

where
\beta
satisfies three conditions for given
r>0
,
\gamma>0
, and
\mu\in[0,1]
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\beta&\geq&\frac{(\nu-2)\gamma}{(\nu-2)\gamma(1-\gamma)-\nu\hat{\kappa}}\Big[\frac{(\nu-1)(1+r)\hat{\kappa}L^{2}\eta^{3}}{(\nu-2)r\mu}+2\rho\Big],\\
\beta&\leq&\frac{(\nu-1)\gamma\eta}{\nu\kappa\mu}\Big[1-\mu-\frac{(1+r)\kappa L^{2}\eta^{2}}{r}\Big],\\
\beta&\leq&\frac{\nu\eta}{\nu+\gamma}.\end{array}\right.

(33)

provided that the right-hand sides of these conditions are well-defined.

Suppose further that (
33
)
holds for a given choice of
r
,
\mu
, and
\gamma
.
Then, the parameters
\tau_{k}
,
\eta
,
\hat{\eta}_{k}
, and
\beta_{k}
satisfy (
30
), and thus (
31
) still holds.

4.3
Convergence Guarantees of (
GFEG
) and Its Special Cases

\mathrm{(a)}

The convergence of (
GFEG
).

Now, we state the main convergence result of (
GFEG
) for a general direction
u^{k}
satisfying the condition (
21
).

Theorem 4.1

For (
NI
), suppose that
\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)\neq\emptyset
,
F
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous,
\Phi
is
\rho
-co-hypomonotone, and
J_{\eta T}
is well-defined and single-valued.
Let
\{(x^{k},y^{k})\}
be generated by (
GFEG
) such that
u^{k}
satisfies (
21
).

Suppose further that
\tau_{k}
,
\hat{\eta}_{k}
, and
\beta_{k}
are updated as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\tau_{k}:=\frac{1}{k+\nu},\quad\hat{\eta}_{k}:=\eta(1-\tau_{k}),\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{k}:=\beta(1-\tau_{k}),\end{array}

(34)

where
\nu>2
is given, and
\eta
and
\beta
are chosen as follows:

\mathrm{(i)}

If
\kappa=0
and
\hat{\kappa}=0
in (
21
), and
2L\rho\leq 1
, then we choose

\begin{array}[]{lcl}0<\eta\leq\frac{1}{L}\quad\text{and}\quad 2\rho\leq\beta<\eta.\end{array}

\mathrm{(ii)}

If
\kappa=0
and
0<\hat{\kappa}<\frac{\nu-2}{4\nu}
in (
21
), and
L\rho<\frac{(\nu-2-4\nu\hat{\kappa})\sigma_{1}}{12(\nu-2)}
, then we choose

\begin{array}[]{lcl}0<\eta\leq\frac{\sigma_{1}}{L}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{2(\nu-2)}{\nu-2-4\nu\hat{\kappa}}\big[\frac{2(\nu-1)\hat{\kappa}\eta\sigma_{1}^{2}}{\nu-2}+2\rho\big]\leq\beta<\frac{2\eta}{3},\end{array}

where
\sigma_{1}^{2}:=\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{\nu-2-4\nu\hat{\kappa}}{12(\nu-1)\hat{\kappa}}\right\}
.

\mathrm{(iii)}

If
\kappa>0
and
\hat{\kappa}=0
in (
21
), and
L\rho\leq\frac{\nu-1}{32\nu\sqrt{\kappa+1}}
, then we choose

\begin{array}[]{lcl}0<\eta\leq\frac{1}{2L\sqrt{\kappa+1}}\quad\text{and}\quad 4\rho\leq\beta\leq\frac{(\nu-1)\eta}{4\nu}.\end{array}

\mathrm{(iv)}

If
\kappa>0
and
0<\hat{\kappa}<\frac{\nu-2}{4\nu}
in (
21
), and
L\rho\leq\frac{(\nu-1)(\nu-2-4\nu\hat{\kappa})\sigma_{2}}{32\nu(\nu-2)}
, then we choose

\begin{array}[]{lcl}0<\eta\leq\frac{1}{2L\sqrt{\kappa+1}}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{2(\nu-2)}{\nu-2-4\nu\hat{\kappa}}\big[\frac{2(\nu-1)(\kappa+1)\hat{\kappa}\eta\sigma_{2}^{2}}{\nu-2}+2\rho\big]\leq\beta\leq\frac{(\nu-1)\eta}{4\nu},\end{array}

where
\sigma_{2}^{2}:=\frac{1}{\kappa+1}\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{\nu-2-4\nu\hat{\kappa}}{32\nu\hat{\kappa}}\right\}
.

Then, in all cases, we have the following bound:

\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|^{2}\leq\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|^{2}+\psi\|Fx^{k}-u^{k}\|^{2}\leq\frac{\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}}{(k+\nu-1)^{2}},

(35)

where
\psi:=0
if
\kappa=\hat{\kappa}=0
, and
\psi:=\frac{8(L^{2}\eta^{3}+\beta)}{2\eta-3\beta}
, otherwise;
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
, and

\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}&\frac{4(\nu-1)}{(\eta-\beta)^{2}}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}+\frac{4(\nu-1)[\eta(\nu-1)-\beta(\nu-2)]}{\eta-\beta}\|Fx^{0}+\xi^{0}\|^{2},&{\!\!\!\!}\textrm{for Case $\mathrm{(i)}$},\\
&\frac{8(\nu-1)}{(2\eta-3\beta)^{2}}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}+\frac{4[2\eta(\nu-1)^{2}-\beta(\nu-2)(2\nu-1)]}{2\eta-3\beta}\|Fx^{0}+\xi^{0}\|^{2},&~\textrm{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Remark 3

Theorem
4.1
proves the
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k^{2}\right)
-last-iterate convergence rate of (
GFEG
) under the condition (
21
).
While the parameter
\kappa>0
is arbitrary,
\hat{\kappa}
must be in a certain range
0\leq\hat{\kappa}<\frac{\nu-2}{4\nu}
.
Letting
\nu\to+\infty
, we get
0\leq\hat{\kappa}<\frac{1}{4}
.
Moreover, we also obtain
L\rho<\frac{1}{32\sqrt{2(\kappa+1)}}\cdot\min\big\{1,\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\hat{\kappa}}}\big\}
when
\kappa>0
.

\mathrm{(b)}

Two special instances.

Now, we consider two special cases of (
GFEG
) when
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
, corresponding to the extra-anchored-gradient (also called fast extragradient) method in
[
40
,
87
]
and
u^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}
, corresponding to the past extra-anchored gradient method in
[
79
]
.

Corollary 2

For (
NI
), suppose that
\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)\neq\emptyset
,
F
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous,
J_{\eta T}
is singled-valued and well-defined, and
\Phi
is
\rho
-co-hypomonotone.
Let
\{(x^{k},y^{k})\}
be generated by (
GFEG
) starting from
x^{0}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)
and using
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
.
Suppose further that
2L\rho\leq 1
, and for any fixed
\nu>2
,
\eta
and
\beta
are respectively chosen such that
\eta\in(0,\frac{1}{L}]
and
\beta\in[2\rho,\eta)
, and

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\tau_{k}:=\frac{1}{k+\nu},\quad\hat{\eta}_{k}:=\eta(1-\tau_{k}),\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{k}:=\beta(1-\tau_{k}).\end{array}

(36)

Then, the following result holds:

\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}}{(\eta-\beta)(k+\nu-1)^{2}},

(37)

where
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}:=\frac{\nu-1}{\eta-\beta}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}+\big[\eta(\nu-1)^{2}-\beta(\nu-2)(\nu-1)\big]\|Fx^{0}+\xi^{0}\|^{2}
.

Proof

Since
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
, (
21
) holds with
\kappa=\hat{\kappa}=0
.
Applying Theorem
4.1
with
\kappa=\hat{\kappa}=0
, we obtain (
36
) from (
34
), and (
35
) reduces to (
37
).

\square

Corollary 3

For (
NI
), suppose that
\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)\neq\emptyset
,
F
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous,
J_{\eta T}
is well-defined, and
\Phi
is
\rho
-co-hypomonotone.
Let
\{(x^{k},y^{k})\}
be generated by (
GFEG
) starting from
x^{0}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)
and
y^{-1}:=x^{0}
using
u^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}
.
Suppose further that for a given
\nu>2
, we have
L\rho\leq\frac{\nu-1}{32\sqrt{2}\nu}
, and
\eta
and
\beta
are respectively chosen such that
\eta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}L}\right]
and
\beta\in\left[4\rho,\frac{(\nu-1)\eta}{4\nu}\right]
, and

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\tau_{k}:=\frac{1}{k+\nu},\quad\hat{\eta}_{k}:=\eta(1-\tau_{k}),\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{k}:=\beta(1-\tau_{k}).\end{array}

(38)

Then, the following result holds:

\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|^{2}\leq\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|^{2}+\psi\cdot\|Fx^{k}-Fy^{k-1}\|^{2}\leq\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}}{(k+\nu-1)^{2}},

(39)

where
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}:=\frac{8(\nu-1)}{(2\eta-3\beta)^{2}}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}+\frac{4[2\eta(\nu-1)^{2}-\beta(\nu-2)(2\nu-1)]}{2\eta-3\beta}\|Fx^{0}+\xi^{0}\|^{2}
for
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
and
\psi:=\frac{8(L^{2}\eta^{3}+\beta)}{2\eta-3\beta}
.

Proof

Since
u^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}
, (
21
) holds with
\kappa=1
and
\hat{\kappa}=0
.
Applying Theorem
4.1
with
\kappa=1
and
\hat{\kappa}=0
, we obtain the update rule (
38
) from (
34
), and (
35
) reduces to (
39
).

\square

Remark 4

The analysis of Theorem
4.1
is much more complicated than that of Theorem
3.1
due to the co-hypomonotonicity of
\Phi
.
In addition, we have not tried to optimally select the parameters in our analysis.
The ranges of
\hat{\kappa}
,
L\rho
, and
\eta
can be improved by refining our analysis, but we omit it here.

5
A Class of Moving Anchor FBFS Methods

Motivation.

The anchored EG-type methods developed in Sections
3
and
4
have at least three aspects that can be improved.

•

First, the fixed anchor point
x^{0}
is propagated over all the iterations.
Although the coefficient
\tau_{k}
of
x^{0}
is decreasing when
k
is growing, this term is still slowing down the actual performance of the algorithm.

•

Second, the update rule
\tau_{k}:=\frac{1}{k+\nu}
of
\tau_{k}
is tight, and has less freedom to improve the practical performance of algorithm.

•

Third, we are unable to prove faster convergence rates (i.e.
o\left(1/k\right)
rates) and the convergence of the iterate sequences (see
[
21
]
).

Here, we explore the ideas of restarting strategies in optimization
[
59
]
and recently moving anchor trick in
[
1
]
to propose a class of moving anchor FBFS methods for solving (
NI
).
Our method can resolve the above three aspects.

5.1
A Class of Moving Anchor FBFS Methods

First, for given
x^{k}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)
and
u^{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we recall the following elements:

w^{k}:=Fx^{k}+\xi^{k},\quad\hat{w}^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}+\xi^{k},\quad\text{and}\quad z^{k}:=u^{k}+\xi^{k},

(40)

for some
\xi^{k}\in Tx^{k}
.

\mathrm{(a)}

The proposed method.

Instead of fixing
x^{0}
, we update
x^{0}
by a forward step similar to those in
[
1
,
88
]
, but using the direction
z^{k}
, not
w^{k+1}
.
More specifically, our algorithm is described as follows.
Starting from
x^{0}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)
, we set
\bar{x}^{0}:=x^{0}
, and at each iteration
k\geq 0
, for given
x^{k}
and
u^{k}
, we update

\hskip-12.91663pt\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}y^{k}&:=&x^{k}+\tau_{k}(\bar{x}^{k}-x^{k})-(\hat{\eta}_{k}-\beta_{k})z^{k},\\
x^{k+1}&:=&x^{k}+\tau_{k}(\bar{x}^{k}-x^{k})-\eta\hat{w}^{k+1}+\beta_{k}z^{k},\\
\bar{x}^{k+1}&:=&\bar{x}^{k}-\gamma z^{k},\end{array}\right.\hskip-12.91663pt

(GFEG
+
)

where
\tau_{k}:=\frac{1}{t_{k}}\in(0,1]
for
t_{k}>1
,
\eta>0
,
\hat{\eta}_{k}>0
,
\beta_{k}\geq 0
, and
\gamma>0
are given parameters, determined later.
The direction
u^{k}
in
z^{k}
from (
40
) is chosen such that it satisfies the following condition:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\|Fx^{k}-u^{k}\|^{2}&\leq&\kappa\|Fx^{k}-Fy^{k-1}\|^{2}+\hat{\kappa}\eta^{2}\|\hat{w}^{k}-(1-\tau_{k-1})w^{k-1}\|^{2},\end{array}

(41)

for given constants
\kappa\geq 0
and
\hat{\kappa}\geq 0
,
y^{-1}=x^{-1}:=x^{0}
, and
u^{0}:=Fx^{0}
.

The scheme (
GFEG+
) looks similar to (
GFEG
), but it has two main differences.
First,
\bar{x}^{k}
is updated by
\bar{x}^{k+1}:=\bar{x}^{k}-\gamma z^{k}
instead of fixing at
\bar{x}^{k}=x^{0}
.
Second, the parameters
\tau_{k}
and
\beta_{k}
are updated differently.

\mathrm{(b)}

Three special instances.

Similar to (
GFEG
), our scheme (
GFEG+
) covers a class of methods by choosing different direction
u^{k}
.
Again, we consider at least three special instances of (
GFEG+
).

\mathrm{(i)}

Variant 1: Moving anchor FBFS method.

If we choose
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
, then (
41
) holds with
\kappa=0
and
\hat{\kappa}=0
.
This variant is similar to the moving anchor extragradient (EAG-V) scheme in
[
1
]
when
T=0
as well as the symplectic extragradient method in
[
88
]
.
However, our last line
\bar{x}^{k+1}:=\bar{x}^{k}-\gamma z^{k}
of (
GFEG+
) is different from
\bar{x}^{k+1}:=\bar{x}^{k}-\gamma w^{k+1}
in
[
1
,
88
]
.
Though this change is not essential in deterministic methods, it is very useful when developing randomized and stochastic variants of (
GFEG+
).

\mathrm{(ii)}

Variant 2: Moving anchor past-FBFS method.

If we choose
u^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}
, then (
41
) holds with
\kappa=1
and
\hat{\kappa}=0
, and (
GFEG+
) reduces to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}y^{k}&:=&\tau_{k}\bar{x}^{k}+(1-\tau_{k})x^{k}-(\hat{\eta}_{k}-\beta_{k})\hat{w}^{k}\\
x^{k+1}&:=&\tau_{k}\bar{x}^{k}+(1-\tau_{k})x^{k}-\eta\hat{w}^{k+1}+\beta_{k}\hat{w}^{k},\\
\bar{x}^{k+1}&:=&\bar{x}^{k}-\gamma\hat{w}^{k}.\end{array}\right.\vskip-2.15277pt

(42)

This scheme is new.
It can be viewed as a variant of the forward-reflected-backward splitting, or an optimistic gradient method in the literature.
One main advantage here is that it saves one evaluation of
F
per iteration.

\mathrm{(iii)}

Variant 3: Generalization.

We can construct a generalized direction:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}u^{k}&:=&\alpha Fx^{k}+(1-\alpha+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\eta}\hat{\alpha})Fy^{k-1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\eta}\hat{\alpha}(1-\tau_{k-1})Fx^{k-1}\\
&&+{~}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\eta}\hat{\alpha}[\xi^{k}-(1-\tau_{k-1})\xi^{k-1}],\end{array}\vskip-2.15277pt

(43)

for any given constants
\alpha,\hat{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Clearly,
u^{k}
is an affine combination of
Fx^{k}
,
Fy^{k-1}
,
Fx^{k-1}
,
\xi^{k}
, and
\xi^{k-1}
.
Moreover, we can verify that
u^{k}
satisfies (
41
) with
\kappa=(1+m)(1-\alpha)^{2}
and
\hat{\kappa}=(1+m^{-1})\hat{\alpha}^{2}
by Young’s inequality for any
m>0
.
Generally,
u^{k}
requires
Fx^{k}
and/or
Fy^{k-1}
, and thus (
GFEG+
) needs at most two evaluations of
F
at each iteration
k
.

\mathrm{(c)}

The implementation of (
GFEG+
)
.

If the resolvent
J_{\eta T}
of
\eta T
is well-defined and single-valued, then we can rewrite (
GFEG+
) equivalently to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}y^{k}&:=&x^{k}+\tau_{k}(\bar{x}^{k}-x^{k})-(\hat{\eta}_{k}-\beta_{k})(u^{k}+\xi^{k}),\\
x^{k+1}&:=&J_{\eta T}\left(y^{k}-\eta Fy^{k}+\hat{\eta}_{k}(u^{k}+\xi^{k})\right),\\
\xi^{k+1}&:=&\frac{1}{\eta}\big[y^{k}+\hat{\eta}_{k}(u^{k}+\xi^{k})-x^{k+1}\big]-Fy^{k},\\
\bar{x}^{k+1}&:=&\bar{x}^{k}-\gamma(u^{k}+\xi^{k}),\end{array}\right.

(44)

where
\xi^{0}\in Tx^{0}
is arbitrary given,
\bar{x}^{0}:=x^{0}
, and
u^{0}:=Fx^{0}
.
In fact, we only require
J_{\eta T}
to be well-defined and not necessarily single-valued.

5.2
Key Estimates for Convergence Analysis

\mathrm{(a)}

Lyapunov function.

To establish the convergence of (
GFEG+
), we define the following functions:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{V}_{k}&:=&\frac{a_{k}}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+t_{k-1}\langle w^{k},x^{k}-\bar{x}^{k}\rangle+\frac{(1-\mu)}{2\gamma}\|\bar{x}^{k}-x^{\star}\|^{2},\\
\mathcal{L}_{k}&:=&\mathcal{V}_{k}+\frac{\lambda\eta t_{k}^{2}}{2}\|u^{k}-Fx^{k}\|^{2},\end{array}

(45)

where
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
,
a_{k}
,
t_{k}
, and
\lambda
are nonnegative parameters, determined later.
Note that
\mathcal{V}_{k}
is similar to the one in (
45
), but
x^{0}
is replaced by
\bar{x}^{k}
.
Again, since we study the generalized scheme (
GFEG+
), we need to add one more term
\frac{\lambda\eta t_{k}^{2}}{2}\|u^{k}-Fx^{k}\|^{2}
to
\mathcal{V}_{k}
to handle the mismatch between
u^{k}
and
Fx^{k}
.

\mathrm{(b)}

Descent property and boundedness.

First, we state the following key lemma for our convergence analysis.

Lemma 5

For (
NI
), suppose that
F
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous and
\Phi
is
\rho
-co-hypomonotone.
Given
\mu\in[0,1)
and
c_{1}\in\big[0,\frac{1}{2}\big)
, let
\{(x^{k},\bar{x}^{k},z^{k})\}
be generated by (
GFEG+
) starting from
x^{0}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)
such that
u^{k}
satisfies (
41
).
Suppose that the parameters are updated as
\tau_{k}:=\frac{1}{t_{k}}
and

\begin{array}[]{lcl}t_{k}:=t_{k-1}+\mu,\quad\hat{\eta}_{k}:=\eta(1-\tau_{k}),\quad\textrm{and}\quad\beta_{k}:=-\gamma\tau_{k}+\frac{2\rho(1-\tau_{k})}{1-2c_{1}}.\end{array}

(46)

Given any
\omega\geq 0
and
c>0
, define
M^{2}:=(1+\omega)(1+c)L^{2}
and
e^{k}:=u^{k}-Fx^{k}
.
Then
\mathcal{V}_{k}
defined by (
45
) satisfies

\hskip-1.07639pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{V}_{k}&\geq&\mathcal{V}_{k+1}+\frac{(a_{k}-\hat{a}_{k})}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{\omega\eta t_{k}^{2}}{2}\|w^{k+1}-\hat{w}^{k+1}\|^{2}\\
&&+{~}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\eta}(1-M^{2}\eta^{2})}{2}\|t_{k}\hat{w}^{k+1}-(t_{k}-1)w^{k}\|^{2}+(1-\mu)\langle w^{k},x^{k}-x^{\star}\rangle\\
&&+{~}(1-\mu)\langle e^{k},\bar{x}^{k}-x^{\star}\rangle-\frac{b_{k}}{2}\|e^{k}\|^{2},\end{array}\hskip-8.61108pt

(47)

where, for any
c_{2}>0
and
\hat{c}>0
,
a_{k+1}
,
\hat{a}_{k}
, and
b_{k}
are respectively given by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}a_{k+1}&:=&t_{k}\big[\eta t_{k}-\frac{2\rho(t_{k}-1)}{1-2c_{1}}\big],\\
\hat{a}_{k}&:=&\eta(t_{k}-1)^{2}+2\gamma(1+c_{2})(t_{k}-1)-\frac{2\rho(t_{k}-1)[t_{k}-2(1+c_{2})]}{1-2c_{1}}\\
&&+{~}(1+\hat{c})(1-\mu)\gamma,\\
b_{k}&:=&\frac{M^{2}\eta^{3}(t_{k}-1)^{2}}{c}+\frac{(t_{k}-1)}{2(1-2c_{1})}\big[\frac{2\rho t_{k}}{c_{1}}+\frac{2\rho+\gamma(1-2c_{1})}{c_{2}}\big]+\frac{(1+\hat{c})(1-\mu)\gamma}{\hat{c}}.\end{array}\right.

(48)

We also need to show that
\mathcal{V}_{k}
in (
45
) is bounded from bellow.

Lemma 6

Let
\mathcal{V}_{k}
be defined by (
45
) and
\Phi
be
\rho
-co-hypomonotone.
Then, for any
\tilde{c}>0
, we have

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{V}_{k}&\geq&\frac{[(1-\mu)\tilde{c}\eta-\gamma]}{2\tilde{c}\gamma\eta}\|\bar{x}^{k}-x^{\star}\|^{2}+\frac{(a_{k}-\tilde{c}\eta t_{k-1}^{2}-2\rho t_{k-1})}{2}\|w^{k}\|^{2}.\end{array}

(49)

5.3
Convergence Analysis for The General Case

\mathrm{(a)}
The
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)
convergence rates.

First, we establish the convergence of (
GFEG+
) for the general case when
u^{k}\neq Fx^{k}
.
Then, we investigate a special case
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
to expand the range of
L\rho
.

Theorem 5.1

For (
NI
), suppose that
\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)\neq\emptyset
,
F
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous, and
\Phi
is
\rho
-co-hypomonotone.
Let
\{(x^{k},y^{k})\}
be generated by (
GFEG+
) such that
u^{k}
satisfies (
41
).
Suppose further that
\tau_{k}
,
\hat{\eta}_{k}
, and
\beta_{k}
are updated as

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\tau_{k}:=\frac{1}{t_{k}}=\frac{1}{\mu(k+r)},\quad\hat{\eta}_{k}:=\eta(1-\tau_{k}),\ \ \text{and}\ \ \beta_{k}:=-\gamma\tau_{k}+4\rho(1-\tau_{k}),\end{array}

(50)

where
r\geq 1+\frac{2}{\mu}
and
\mu\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
are given, and
\eta
and
\gamma
are chosen such that

\begin{array}[]{ll}8\rho<\eta\leq\bar{\eta}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2(1+4\kappa)L^{2}+4\hat{\kappa}}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\gamma:=\frac{(1-2\mu)\eta}{6},\end{array}

(51)

provided that
128[(1+4\kappa)L^{2}+2\hat{\kappa}]\rho^{2}<1
.
Then, we have

\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|^{2}\leq\frac{6e^{\frac{\theta}{r}}\cdot\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}}{\eta\mu^{2}(k+r-1)^{2}},

(52)

where
\theta:=\frac{(1-2\mu)(1-\mu)^{2}}{2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nu}(2-\mu)\mu^{2}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}:=\frac{\eta\mu^{2}(r-1)^{2}}{2}\|Fx^{0}+\xi^{0}\|^{2}+\frac{3(1-\mu)}{(1-2\mu)\eta}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}
.
Moreover, we also have

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}[\mu(k+r)-1]\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|^{2}&\leq&\frac{4\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}}{(3-2\mu)(\eta-8\rho)}\big(1+\frac{\theta}{r}e^{\frac{\theta}{r}}\big).\end{array}

(53)

The choice of
\eta
and
\gamma
and the update rule of
\beta_{k}
can be improved to get a larger range by optimizing the constants in Lemma
5
.
In Theorem
5.1
, we only provide one choice of these constants, which may not be the best one.
For instance, if
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
, then we have
\kappa=\hat{\kappa}=0
, and the range of
L\rho
in Theorem
5.1
reduces to
8\sqrt{2}L\rho<1
, which is much worse than
2L\rho<1
stated in Theorem
5.3
below.
Though Theorem
5.1
provides a unified result for all choices of
u^{k}
satisfying (
41
), for specific choice of
u^{k}
, it is still possible to improve the range of parameters.
Let us consider the following examples.

\mathrm{(i)}

If
u^{k}:=Fx^{k}
, then we separate its analysis in Subsection
5.4
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

If
u^{k}:=Fy^{k-1}
, then
\kappa=1
and
\hat{\kappa}=0
, the range of
L\rho
reduces to
8\sqrt{10}L\rho<1
.
Certainly, this range can be further improved by refining our analysis in Theorem
5.1
.

\mathrm{(iii)}

If
\rho=0
, i.e.
\Phi
is monotone, then we can improve the range of
\eta
and
\gamma
respectively to, e.g.,
0<\eta\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2(1+3\kappa)L^{2}+3\hat{\kappa}}}
and
\gamma:=\frac{(1-\mu)\eta}{3}
.

\mathrm{(b)}
The
o\big(1/k^{2}\big)
Convergence Rates and Convergence of Iterates.

Now, we prove
o\big(1/k^{2}\big)
convergence rates of (
GFEG+
) and the convergence of iterate sequences to a solution of (
NI
).

Theorem 5.2

Under the conditions and settings of Theorem
5.1
, we additionally assume that
\eta<\bar{\eta}
.
Then

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+r)^{2}\|Fx^{k+1}-Fy^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+r)^{2}\|u^{k}-Fx^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+r)\|y^{k}-x^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty,\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+r)\|x^{k+1}-x^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty.\end{array}

(54)

We also have

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|^{2}&=&0,\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\|y^{k}-x^{k}\|^{2}}&=&0,\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\|x^{k+1}-x^{k}\|^{2}}&=&0.\end{array}

(55)

Moreover, if
T
is closed, then all three sequences
\{x^{k}\}
,
\{y^{k}\}
, and
\{\bar{x}^{k}\}
converge to a solution
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
of (
NI
).

The limits in (
55
) show that
\|Fx^{k}+\xi^{k}\|^{2}=o\big(1/k^{2}\big)
,
\|x^{k+1}-x^{k}\|^{2}=o\big(1/k^{2}\big)
, and
\|y^{k}-x^{k}\|^{2}=o\big(1/k^{2}\big)
, stating a faster convergence rate than
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)
in Theorem
5.1
.
However, this faster rate is achieved when
k
is sufficiently large, while the
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)
bound holds for all
k\geq 0
.

Since
F
is continuous, the closedness of
T
implies the closedness of
\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)
, which guarantees that if
(x^{k},v^{k})\in\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)
and
(x^{k},v^{k})
converges to
(x,v)
, then
(x,v)\in\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)
.
In particular, this condition holds if
T
is maximally monotone
(
9
, Proposition 20.38)
.

5.4
Special Case – Moving Anchor Extragradient Method

The choice
